{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/discover-important-function\n\n# Fuzz Target Localizer\n\n## Purpose\n\nThis skill helps an agent quickly narrow a Python repository down to a small set of high-value fuzz targets. It produces:\n\n- A ranked list of important files\n- A ranked list of important functions and methods\n- A summary of existing unit tests and inferred oracles\n- A final shortlist of functions-under-test (FUTs) for fuzzing, each with a structured \"note to self\" for follow-up actions\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this skill when the user asks to:\n\n- Find the best functions to fuzz in a Python package/library\n- Identify parsers, decoders, validators, or boundary code that is fuzz-worthy\n- Decide what to fuzz based on existing test coverage and API surface\n- Produce a structured shortlist of fuzz targets with harness guidance\n- Automating testing pipeline setup for a new or existing Python project.\n\nDo not use this skill when the user primarily wants to fix a specific bug,\nrefactor code, or implement a harness immediately\n(unless they explicitly ask for target selection first).\n\n## Inputs expected from the environment\n\nThe agent should assume access to:\n\n- Repository filesystem\n- Ability to read files\n- Ability to run local commands (optional but recommended)\n\nPreferred repository listing command:\n\n- Use `tree` to get a fast, high-signal view of repository structure (limit depth if needed).\n\nIf `tree` is not available, fall back to a recursive listing via other standard shell tooling.\n\n## Outputs\n\nProduce result:\n\nA report in any format with the following information: - Localize important files - Localize important functions - Summarize existing unit tests - Decide function under test for fuzzing\nThis file should be placed in the root of the repository as `APIs.txt`,\nwhich servers as the guidelines for future fuzzing harness implementation.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Prefer analysis and reporting over code changes.\n- Do not modify source code unless the user explicitly requests changes.\n- If running commands could be disruptive, default to read-only analysis.\n- Avoid assumptions about runtime behavior; base conclusions on code and tests.\n- Explicitly consider existing tests in the repo when selecting targets and deciding harness shape.\n- Keep the final FUT shortlist small (typically 1–5).\n\n---\n\n# Localize important files\n\n## Goal\n\nProduce a ranked list of files that are most likely to contain fuzz-worthy logic: parsing, decoding, deserialization, validation, protocol handling, file/network boundaries, or native bindings.\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Build a repository map\n    - Start with a repository overview using `tree` to identify:\n        - Root packages and layouts (src/ layout vs flat layout)\n        - Test directories and configs\n        - Bindings/native directories\n        - Examples, docs, and tooling directories\n    - Identify packaging and metadata files such as:\n        - pyproject.toml\n        - setup.cfg\n        - setup.py\n    - Identify test configuration and entry points:\n        - tests/\n        - conftest.py\n        - pytest.ini\n        - tox.ini\n        - noxfile.py\n\n2. Exclude low-value areas\n    - Skip: virtual environments, build outputs, vendored code, documentation-only directories, examples-only directories, generated files.\n\n3. Score files using explainable heuristics\n   Assign a file a higher score when it matches more of these indicators:\n    - Public API exposure: **init**.py re-exports, **all**, api modules\n    - Input boundary keywords in file path or symbols: parse, load, dump, decode, encode, deserialize, serialize, validate, normalize, schema, protocol, message\n    - Format handlers: json, yaml, xml, csv, toml, protobuf, msgpack, pickle\n    - Regex-heavy or templating-heavy code\n    - Native boundaries: ctypes, cffi, cython, extension modules, bindings\n    - Central modules: high import fan-in across the package\n    - Test adjacency: directly imported or heavily referenced by tests\n\n4. Rank and select\n    - Produce a Top-N list (default 10–30) with a short rationale per file.\n\n## Output format\n\nFor each file:\n\n- path\n- score (relative, not necessarily normalized)\n- rationale (2–5 bullets)\n- indicators_hit (list)\n\n---\n\n# Localize important functions\n\n## Goal\n\nFrom the important files, identify and rank functions or methods that are strong fuzz targets while minimizing full-body reading until needed.\n\n## Approach 1: AST-based header and docstring scan\n\nFor each localized Python file, parse it using Python’s `ast` module and extract only:\n\n- Module docstring\n- Function and async function headers (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class headers and method headers\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nDo not read full function bodies during the initial pass unless needed for disambiguation or final selection.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Build per-file declarations via AST\n    - Parse the file with `ast`\n    - Enumerate:\n        - Top-level functions\n        - Classes and their methods\n        - Nested functions only if they are likely to be directly fuzzable via an exposed wrapper\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Signature details (as available from AST)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Location information (file, line range if available)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using headers and docstrings\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Read important files sequentially\n    - For each file from the important files list, read the full contents.\n    - Extract by direct inspection:\n        - Top-level functions and their complete implementations\n        - Classes and their methods with full bodies\n        - Nested functions if they are exposed or called by public APIs\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Complete signature (from source text)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Full function body\n        - Location information (file, approximate line range)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using full source analysis\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Contain complex control flow, loops, or recursive calls\n    - Handle exceptions or edge cases\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Analyze implementation details from full bodies\n   For each candidate function, inspect the body for:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions (explicit checks, assertions, early returns)\n    - Internal helpers called and their purposes\n    - Error handling style and exception types raised\n    - Invariants and postconditions (explicit or implicit)\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n    - Input transformations and data flow\n    - Native calls or external process invocations\n    - Resource allocation and cleanup patterns\n\n4. Use tests to refine candidate selection\n    - Check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n    - Note which aspects of each function are tested vs untested.\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from full body analysis)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n    - Observable implementation risks (unsafe operations, unchecked inputs, complex state)\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n- implementation_notes (key observations from body analysis)\n\n---\n\n# Summarize existing unit tests\n\n## Goal\n\nSummarize what is already tested, infer test oracles, and identify gaps that fuzzing can complement.\n\n## Hard requirement\n\nAlways inspect and incorporate existing tests in the repository when:\n\n- Ranking functions\n- Selecting FUTs\n- Designing input models and oracles\n- Proposing seed corpus sources\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Inventory tests\n    - Locate tests and their discovery configuration (pytest.ini, pyproject.toml, tox.ini, noxfile.py).\n    - Enumerate test modules and map them to source modules via imports.\n    - Identify shared fixtures and data factories (conftest.py, fixture files, test utilities).\n\n2. Summarize test intent\n   For each test module:\n    - What behaviors are asserted\n    - What inputs are used\n    - What exceptions are expected\n    - What invariants are implied\n\n3. Infer oracles and properties\n   Common fuzz-friendly oracles include:\n    - Round-trip properties\n    - Idempotence of normalization\n    - Parser consistency across equivalent inputs\n    - Deterministic output given deterministic input\n    - No-crash and no-hang for malformed inputs\n\n4. Identify coverage gaps\n    - FUT candidates with no direct tests\n    - Input classes not covered by tests (size extremes, malformed encodings, deep nesting, edge unicode, invalid schemas)\n    - Code paths guarded by complex conditionals or exception handlers with no tests\n\n## Output format\n\n- test_map: module_under_test → tests → asserted behaviors\n- inferred_oracles: list of reusable invariants with the functions they apply to\n- gaps: ranked list of untested or weakly-tested candidates\n\n---\n\n# Decide function under test for fuzzing\n\n## Goal\n\nSelect a final set of FUTs (typically 1–5) and produce a structured \"note to self\" for each FUT so the agent can proceed to harness implementation, corpus seeding, and fuzz execution.\n\n## Selection criteria\n\nPrefer FUTs that maximize:\n\n- Security/robustness payoff (parsing, decoding, validation, native boundary)\n- Reachability with minimal setup\n- Low existing test assurance or narrow test input coverage\n- High fuzzability (simple input channel, clear oracle or crash-only target)\n\nExplicitly weigh:\n\n- What tests already cover (and what they do not)\n- What seeds can be extracted from tests, fixtures, and sample data\n\n## Required \"note to self\" template\n\nFor each selected FUT, produce exactly this structure:\n\nFuzzing Target Note\n\n- Target:\n- File / location:\n- Why this target:\n    - Input surface:\n    - Boundary or native considerations:\n    - Complexity or path depth:\n    - Current test gaps:\n- Callable contract:\n    - Required imports or initialization:\n    - Preconditions:\n    - Determinism concerns:\n    - External dependencies:\n- Input model:\n    - Primary payload type:\n    - Decoding or parsing steps:\n    - Constraints to respect:\n    - Edge classes to emphasize:\n- Oracles:\n    - Must-hold properties:\n    - Acceptable exceptions:\n    - Suspicious exceptions:\n    - Crash-only vs correctness-checking:\n- Harness plan:\n    - Recommended approach:\n    - Minimal harness signature:\n    - Seed corpus ideas:\n    - Timeouts and resource limits:\n- Risk flags:\n    - Native extension involved:\n    - Potential DoS paths:\n    - External I/O:\n- Next actions:\n    1.\n    2.\n    3.\n    4.\n\n## Output format\n\n- selected_futs: list of chosen FUTs with brief justification\n- notes_to_self: one \"Fuzzing Target Note\" per FUT\n\n---\n\n# Final JSON block\n\nAt the end of the report, include a JSON object with:\n\n- important_files: [{path, score, rationale, indicators_hit}]\n- important_functions: [{qualname, file, line_range, score, rationale, dependencies, harnessability}]\n- test_summary: {test_map, inferred_oracles, gaps}\n- selected_futs: [{qualname, file, line_range, justification}]\n- notes_to_self: [{target_qualname, note}]\n\n\nARGUMENTS: --target /app/arrow"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"6cf3fee6-1081-4147-b0b7-48e6c0673688","uuid":"93cfde96-41c9-4608-b5f1-91470cba3306","isSynthetic":true}

def __init__(self, locale: str = DEFAULT_LOCALE, cache_size: int = 0) -> None:\n   210→        \"\"\"\n   211→        Contains the regular expressions and functions to parse and split the input    # TODO: since we support more than ISO 8601, we should rename this function\n   251→    # IDEA: break into multiple functions\n   252→    def parse_iso(\n   253→        self, datetime_string: str,            datetime_string = re.sub(r\"\\s+\", \" \", datetime_string.strip())\n   274→\n   275→        has_space_divider = \" \" in datetime_string\n   276→        has_t_divider = \"T\" in 283→            )\n   284→\n   285→        has_time = has_space_divider or has_t_divider\n   286→        has_tz = False\n   287→\n   288→        # date formats (ISO 8601 and others) to test against\n   289→        # NOTE: YYYYMM is omitted to avoid confusion with YYMMDD (no longer part of ISO 8601, but is still often used)\n   290→        formats = [\n   291→            \"YYYY-MM-DD\",\n   292→            \"YYYY-M-DD\",\n   293→            \"YYYY-M-D\",\n   294→            \"YYYY/MM/DD\",\n   295→            \"YYYY/M/DD\",\n   296→            \"YYYY/M/D\",\n   297→            \"YYYY.MM.DD\",\n datetime_string.split(\"T\", 1)\n   315→\n   316→            time_parts = re.split(\n   317→                r\"[\\+\\-Z]\", time_string, maxsplit=1, flags=re.IGNORECASE\n   318→            )\n   319→\n   320→            time_components: Optional[Match[str]] = self._TIME_RE.match(time_parts[0])\n   321→\n   322→            if time_components is None:\n   323→                raise ParserError(\n   324→                    \"Invalid time component provided. \"\n   325→                    \"Please specify a format or provide a valid time component in the basic or extended ISO 8601 time format.\"\n   326→                )\n   327→\n   328→            (\n   329→                hours,\n   330→                minutes,\n   331→                seconds,\n   332→                subseconds_sep,\n   333→                subseconds,\n   334→            ) = time_components.groups()\n   335→\n   336→            has_tz = len(time_parts) == 2\n   337→            has_minutes = minutes is not None\n   338→            has_seconds = seconds is not None\n   339→            has_subseconds = subseconds is not None\n   340→\n   341→            is_basic_time_format = \":\" not in 371→        return self._parse_multiformat(datetime_string, formats)\n   372→\n   373→    def parse(\n   374→        self,\n   375→        datetime_string: str,\n   376→        fmt: Union[List[str], str],\n   377→        normalize_whitespace: bool = False,\n   378→    ) -> datetime:\n   379→        \"\"\"\n   380→        Parses a datetime string using a specified format.\n   381→\n   382→        :param datetime_string: The datetime string to parse.\n   383→        :param fmt: The format string or list of format strings to use for parsing.\n   384→        :param normalize_whitespace: Whether to normalize whitespace in the datetime string (default is False).\n   385→        :type datetime_string: str\n   386→        :type fmt: Union[List[str], str]\n   387→        :type normalize_whitespace: bool\n   388→        :returns: The parsed datetime object.\n   389→        :rtype: datetime\n   390→        :raises ParserMatchError: If the datetime string does not match the specified format.\n   391→\n   392→        Usage::\n   393→\n   394→        >>> import arrow.parser\n   395→        >>> arrow.parser.DateTimeParser().parse('2021-10-12 14:30:00', 'YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss')\n   396→        datetime.datetime(2021, 10, 12, 14, 30)\n   397→\n   398→\n   399→        \"\"\"\n   400→        if normalize_whitespace:\n   401→            datetime_string = re.sub(r\"\\s+\", \" \", datetime_string)\n   402→\n   403→        if isinstance(fmt, list):\n   404→            return self._parse_multiformat(datetime_string, fmt)\n   405→\n   406→        try:\n   407→            fmt_tokens: List[_FORMAT_TYPE]\n   408→            fmt_pattern_re: Pattern[str]\n   409→            fmt_tokens, fmt_pattern_re = self._generate_pattern_re(fmt)\n   410→        except re.error as e:\n   411→            raise ParserMatchError(\n   412→                f\"Failed to generate regular expression pattern: {e}.\"\n   413→            )\n   414→\n   415→        match = fmt_pattern_re.search(datetime_string)\n   416→\n   417→        if match is None:\n   418→            raise ParserMatchError(\n   419→                f\"Failed to match {fmt!r} when parsing {datetime_string!r}.\"\n   420→            )\n   421→\n   422→        parts: _Parts = {}\n   423→        for token in fmt_tokens:\n   424→            value: Union[Tuple[str, str, str], str]\n   425→ ParserMatchError(\n   434→                    f\"Unable to find a match group for the specified token {token!r}.\"\n   435→                )\n   436→\n   437→            self._parse_token(token, value, parts)  # type: ignore[arg-type]\n   438→\n   439→        return self._build_datetime(parts)\n   440→\n   441→    def _generate_pattern_re(self, fmt: str) -> Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]:\n   442→        \"\"\"\n   443→        Generates a regular expression pattern from a format string.\n   444→\n   445→        :param fmt: The format string to convert into a regular expression pattern.\n   446→        :type fmt: str\n   447→        :returns: A tuple containing a list of format tokens and the corresponding regular expression pattern.\n   448→        :rtype: Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]\n   449→        :raises ParserError: If an unrecognized token is encountered in the format string.\n   450→        \"\"\"\n   451→        # fmt is a string of tokens like 'YYYY-MM-DD'\n   452→        # we construct a new string by replacing each\n   453→        # token by its pattern:\n   454→        # 'YYYY-MM-DD' -> '(?P<YYYY>\\d{4})-(?P<MM>\\d{2})-(?P<DD>\\d{2})'\n   455→        tokens: List[_FORMAT_TYPE] = []\n   456→        offset = 0\n   457→\n   458→        # Escape all special RegEx chars\n   459→        escaped_fmt = re.escape(fmt)\n   460→\n   461→        # Extract the bracketed expressions to be reinserted later.\n   462→        escaped_fmt = re.sub(self._ESCAPE_RE, \"#\", escaped_fmt)\n   463→\n   464→        # Any number of S is the same as one.\n   465→        # TODO: allow users to specify the number of digits to parse\n   466→        escaped_fmt = re.sub(r\"S+\", \"S\", escaped_fmt)\n   467→\n   468→        escaped_data = re.findall(self._ESCAPE_RE, fmt)\n   469→\n   470→        fmt_pattern = escaped_fmt\n   471→\n   472→        for m in self._FORMAT_RE.finditer(escaped_fmt):\n   473→            token: _FORMAT_TYPE = cast(_FORMAT_TYPE, m.group(0))\n   474→            try:\n   475→                input_re = self._input_re_map[token]\n   476→            except KeyError:\n   477→                raise ParserError(f\"Unrecognized token {token!r}.\")\n   478→            input_pattern = f\"(?P<{token}>{input_re.pattern})\"\n   479→            tokens.append(token)\n   480→            # a pattern doesn't have the same length as the token\n   481→            # it replaces! We keep the difference in the offset variable.\n   482→            # This works because the string is scanned left-to-right and matches\n   483→            # are returned in the order found by finditer.\n   484→            fmt_pattern = (\n   485→                fmt_pattern[: m.start() + offset]\n   486→                + input_pattern\n   487→                + fmt_pattern[m.end() + offset :]\n   488→            )\n   489→            offset += len(input_pattern) - (m.end() - m.start())\n   490→\n   491→        final_fmt_pattern = \"\"\n   492→        split_fmt = fmt_pattern.split(r\"\\#\")\n   493→\n   494→        # Due to the way Python splits, 'split_fmt' will always be longer\n   495→        for i in range(len(split_fmt)):\n   496→            final_fmt_pattern += split_fmt[i]\n   497→            if i < len(escaped_data):\n   498→                final_fmt_pattern += escaped_data[i][1:-1]\n   499→\n   500→        # Wrap final_fmt_pattern in a custom word boundary to strictly\n   501→        # match the formatting pattern and filter out date and time formats\n   502→        # that include junk such as: blah1998-09-12 blah, blah 1998-09-12blah,\n   503→        # blah1998-09-12blah. The custom word boundary matches every character\n   504→        # that is not a whitespace character to allow for searching for a date\n   505→        # and time string in a natural language sentence. Therefore, searching\n   506→        # for a string of the form YYYY-MM-DD in \"blah 1998-09-12 blah\" will\n   507→        # work properly.\n   508→        # Certain punctuation before or after the target pattern such as\n   509→        # \"1998-09-12,\" is permitted. For the full list of valid punctuation,\n   510→        # see the documentation.\n   511→\n   512→        starting_word_boundary = (\n   513→            r\"(?<!\\S\\S)\"  # Don't have two consecutive non-whitespace characters. This ensures that we allow cases\n   514→            # like .11.25.2019 but not 1.11.25.2019 (for pattern MM.DD.YYYY)\n   515→            r\"(?<![^\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)<>\\s])\"  # This is the list of punctuation that is ok before the\n   516→            # pattern (i.e. \"It can't not be these characters before the pattern\")\n   517→            r\"(\\b|^)\"\n   518→            # The \\b is to block cases like 1201912 but allow 201912 for pattern YYYYMM. The ^ was necessary to allow a\n   519→            # negative number through i.e. before epoch numbers\n   520→        )\n   521→        ending_word_boundary = (\n   522→            r\"(?=[\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)\\<\\>]?\"  # Positive lookahead stating that these punctuation marks\n   523→            # can appear after the pattern at most 1 time\n   524→            r\"(?!\\S))\"  # Don't allow any non-whitespace character after the punctuation\n   525→        )\n   526→        bounded_fmt_pattern = r\"{}{}{}\".format(\n   527→            starting_word_boundary, final_fmt_pattern, ending_word_boundary\n   528→        )\n   529→\n   530→        return tokens, re.compile(bounded_fmt_pattern, flags=re.IGNORECASE)\n   531→\n   532→    @overload\n   533→    def _parse_token(\n   534→        self,\n   535→        token: Literal[\n   536→            \"YYYY\",\n   537→            \"YY\",\n   538→            \"MM\",\n   539→            \"M\",\n   540→            \"DDDD\",\n   541→            \"DDD\",\n   542→            \"DD\",\n   543→            \"D\",\n   544→            \"Do\",\n   545→            \"HH\",\n   546→            \"hh\",\n   547→            \"h\",\n   548→            \"H\",\n   549→            \"mm\",\n   550→            \"m\",\n   551→            \"ss\",\n   552→            \"s\",\n   553→            \"x\",\n   554→        ],\n   555→        value: Union[str, bytes, SupportsInt, bytearray],\n   556→        parts: _Parts,\n   557→    ) -> None: ...  # pragma: no cover\n   582→\n   583→    @overload\n   584→    def _parse_token(\n   585→        self,\n   586→        token: Literal[\"W\"],\n   587→        value: Tuple[_WEEKDATE_ELEMENT, _WEEKDATE_ELEMENT, Optional[_WEEKDATE_ELEMENT]],\n   588→        parts: _Parts,\n   589→    ) -> None: ...  # pragma: no cover\n   590→\n   591→    def _parse_token(\n   592→        self,\n   593→        token: Any,\n   594→        value: Any,\n   595→        parts: _Parts,\n   596→    ) -> None:\n   597→        \"\"\"\n   598→        Parse a token and its value, and update the `_Parts` dictionary with the parsed values.\n   599→\n   600→        The function supports several tokens, including \"YYYY\", \"YY\", \"MMMM\", \"MMM\", \"MM\", \"M\", \"DDDD\", \"DDD\", \"DD\", \"D\", \"Do\", \"dddd\", \"ddd\", \"HH\", \"H\", \"mm\", \"m\", \"ss\", \"s\", \"S\", \"X\", \"x\", \"ZZZ\", \"ZZ\", \"Z\", \"a\", \"A\", and \"W\". Each token is matched and the corresponding value is parsed and added to the `_Parts` dictionary.\n   601→\n   602→        :param token: The token to parse.\n   603→        :type token: Any\n   604→        :param value: The value of the token.\n   605→        :type value: Any\n   606→        :param parts: A dictionary to update with the parsed values.\n   607→        :type parts: _Parts\n   608→        :raises ParserMatchError: If the hour token value is not between 0 and 12 inclusive for tokens \"a\" or \"A\".\n   609→\n   610→        \"\"\"\n   611→        if token == \"YYYY\":\n   612→            parts[\"year\"] = int(value)\n   613→\n   614→        elif token == \"YY\":\n   615→            value = int(value)\n   616→            parts[\"year\"] = 1900 + value if value > 68 else 2000 + value\n   617→\n   618→        elif token in [\"MMMM\", \"MMM\"]:\n   619→            # FIXME: month_number() is nullable\n   620→            parts[\"month\"] = self.locale.month_number(value.lower())  # type: ignore[typeddict-item]\n   621→\n   622→        elif token in [\"MM\", \"M\"]:\n   623→            parts[\"month\"] = int(value)\n   624→\n   625→        elif token in [\"DDDD\", \"DDD\"]:\n   626→            parts[\"day_of_year\"] = int(value)\n   627→\n   628→        elif token in [\"DD\", \"D\"]:\n   629→            parts[\"day\"] = int(value)\n   630→\n   631→        elif token == \"Do\":\n   632→            parts[\"day\"] = int(value)\n   633→\n   634→        elif token == \"dddd\":\n   635→            # locale day names are 1-indexed\n   636→            day_of_week = [x.lower() for x in self.locale.day_names].index(\n   637→                value.lower()\n   638→            )\n   639→            parts[\"day_of_week\"] = day_of_week - 1\n   640→\n   641→        elif token == \"ddd\":\n   642→            # locale day abbreviations are 1-indexed\n   643→            day_of_week = [x.lower() for x in self.locale.day_abbreviations].index(\n   644→                value.lower()\n   645→            )\n   646→            parts[\"day_of_week\"] = day_of_week - 1\n   647→\n   648→        elif token.upper() in [\"HH\", \"H\"]:\n   649→            parts[\"hour\"] = int(value)\n   650→\n   651→        elif token in [\"mm\", \"m\"]:\n   652→            parts[\"minute\"] = int(value)\n   653→\n   654→        elif token in [\"ss\", \"s\"]:\n   655→            parts[\"second\"] = int(value)\n   656→\n   657→        elif token == \"S\":\n   658→            # We have the *most significant* digits of an arbitrary-precision integer.\n   659→            # We want the six most significant digits as an integer, rounded.\n   660→            # IDEA: add nanosecond support somehow? Need datetime support for it first.\n   661→            value = value.ljust(7, \"0\")\n   662→\n   663→            # floating-point (IEEE-754) defaults to half-to-even rounding\n   664→            seventh_digit = int(value[6])\n   665→            if seventh_digit == 5:\n   666→                rounding = int(value[5]) % 2\n   667→            elif seventh_digit > 5:\n   668→                rounding = 1\n   669→            else:\n   670→                rounding = 0\n   671→\n   672→            parts[\"microsecond\"] = int(value[:6]) + rounding\n   673→\n   674→        elif token == \"X\":\n   675→            parts[\"timestamp\"] = float(value)\n   676→\n   677→        elif token == \"x\":\n   678→            parts[\"expanded_timestamp\"] = int(value)\n   679→\n   680→        elif token in [\"ZZZ\", \"ZZ\", \"Z\"]:\n   681→            parts[\"tzinfo\"] = TzinfoParser.parse(value)\n   682→\n   683→        elif token in [\"a\", \"A\"]:\n   684→            if value in (self.locale.meridians[\"am\"], self.locale.meridians[\"AM\"]):\n   685→                parts[\"am_pm\"] = \"am\"\n   686→                if \"hour\" in parts and not 0 <= parts[\"hour\"] <= 12:\n   687→                    raise ParserMatchError(\n   688→                        f\"Hour token value must be between 0 and 12 inclusive for token {token!r}.\"\n   689→                    )\n   690→            elif value in (self.locale.meridians[\"pm\"], self.locale.meridians[\"PM\"]):\n   691→                parts[\"am_pm\"] = \"pm\"\n   692→        elif token == \"W\":\n   693→            parts[\"weekdate\"] = value\n   694→\n   695→    @staticmethod\n   696→    def _build_datetime(parts: _Parts) -> datetime:\n   697→        \"\"\"\n   698→        Build a datetime object from a dictionary of date parts.\n   699→\n   700→        :param parts: A dictionary containing the date parts extracted from a date string.\n   701→        :type parts: dict\n   702→        :return: A datetime object representing the date and time.\n   703→        :rtype: datetime.datetime\n   704→        \"\"\"\n   705→        weekdate = parts.get(\"weekdate\")\n   706→\n   707→        if weekdate is not None:\n   708→            year, week = int(weekdate[0]), int(weekdate[1])\n   709→\n   710→            if weekdate[2] is not None:\n   711→                _day = int(weekdate[2])\n   712→            else:\n   713→                # day not given, default to 1\n   714→                _day = 1\n   715→\n   716→            date_string = f\"{year}-{week}-{_day}\"\n   717→\n   718→            #  tokens for ISO 8601 weekdates\n   719→            dt = datetime.strptime(date_string, \"%G-%V-%u\")\n   720→\n   721→            parts[\"year\"] = dt.year\n   722→            parts[\"month\"] = dt.month\n   723→            parts[\"day\"] = dt.day\n   724→\n   725→        timestamp = parts.get(\"timestamp\")\n   726→\n   727→        if timestamp is not None:\n   728→            return Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Generates a regular expression pattern from a format string.\n\n        :param fmt: The format string to convert into a regular expression pattern.\n        :type fmt: str\n        :returns: A tuple containing a list of format tokens and the corresponding regular expression pattern.\n        :rtype: Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]\n        :raises ParserError: If an unrecognized token is encountered in the format string.\n        \"\"\"\n        # fmt is a string of tokens like 'YYYY-MM-DD'\n        # we construct a new string by replacing each\n        # token by its pattern:\n        # 'YYYY-MM-DD' -> '(?P<YYYY>\\d{4})-(?P<MM>\\d{2})-(?P<DD>\\d{2})'\n        tokens: List[_FORMAT_TYPE] = []\n        offset = 0\n\n        # Escape all special RegEx chars\n        escaped_fmt = re.escape(fmt)\n\n        # Extract the bracketed expressions to be reinserted later.\n        escaped_fmt = re.sub(self._ESCAPE_RE, \"#\", escaped_fmt)\n\n        # Any number of S is the same as one.\n        # TODO: allow users to specify the number of digits to parse\n        escaped_fmt = re.sub(r\"S+\", \"S\", escaped_fmt)\n\n        escaped_data = re.findall(self._ESCAPE_RE, fmt)\n\n        fmt_pattern = escaped_fmt\n\n        for m in self._FORMAT_RE.finditer(escaped_fmt):\n            token: _FORMAT_TYPE = cast(_FORMAT_TYPE, m.group(0))\n            try:\n                input_re = self._input_re_map[token]\n            except KeyError:\n                raise ParserError(f\"Unrecognized token {token!r}.\")\n            input_pattern = f\"(?P<{token}>{input_re.pattern})\"\n            tokens.append(token)\n            # a pattern doesn't have the same length as the token\n            # it replaces! We keep the difference in the offset variable.\n            # This works because the string is scanned left-to-right and matches\n            # are returned in the order found by finditer.\n            fmt_pattern = (\n                fmt_pattern[: m.start() + offset]\n                + input_pattern\n                + fmt_pattern[m.end() + offset :]\n            )\n            offset += len(input_pattern) - (m.end() - m.start())\n\n        final_fmt_pattern = \"\"\n        split_fmt = fmt_pattern.split(r\"\\#\")\n\n        # Due to the way Python splits, 'split_fmt' will always be longer\n        for i in range(len(split_fmt)):\n            final_fmt_pattern += split_fmt[i]\n            if i < len(escaped_data):\n                final_fmt_pattern += escaped_data[i][1:-1]\n\n        # Wrap final_fmt_pattern in a custom word boundary to strictly\n        # match the formatting pattern and filter out date and time formats\n        # that include junk such as: blah1998-09-12 blah, blah 1998-09-12blah,\n        # blah1998-09-12blah. The custom word boundary matches every character\n        # that is not a whitespace character to allow for searching for a date\n        # and time string in a natural language sentence. Therefore, searching\n        # for a string of the form YYYY-MM-DD in \"blah 1998-09-12 blah\" will\n        # work properly.\n        # Certain punctuation before or after the target pattern such as\n        # \"1998-09-12,\" is permitted. For the full list of valid punctuation,\n        # see the documentation.\n\n        starting_word_boundary = (\n            r\"(?<!\\S\\S)\"  # Don't have two consecutive non-whitespace characters. This ensures that we allow cases\n            # like .11.25.2019 but not 1.11.25.2019 (for pattern MM.DD.YYYY)\n            r\"(?<![^\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)<>\\s])\"  # This is the list of punctuation that is ok before the\n            # pattern (i.e. \"It can't not be these characters before the pattern\")\n            r\"(\\b|^)\"\n            # The \\b is to block cases like 1201912 but allow 201912 for pattern YYYYMM. The ^ was necessary to allow a\n            # negative number through i.e. before epoch numbers\n        )\n        ending_word_boundary = (\n            r\"(?=[\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)\\<\\>]?\"  # Positive lookahead stating that these punctuation marks\n            # can appear after the pattern at most 1 time\n            r\"(?!\\S))\"  # Don't allow any non-whitespace character after the punctuation\n        )\n        bounded_fmt_pattern = r\"{}{}{}\".format(\n            starting_word_boundary, final_fmt_pattern, ending_word_boundary\n        )\n\n        return tokens, re.compile(bounded_fmt_pattern, flags=re.IGNORECASE)\n\n    @overload\n    def _parse_token(\n        self,\n        token: Literal[\n            \"YYYY\",\n            \"YY\",\n            \"MM\",\n            \"M\",\n            \"DDDD\",\n            \"DDD\",\n            \"DD\",\n            \"D\",\n            \"Do\",\n            \"HH\",\n            \"hh\",\n            \"h\",\n            \"H\",\n            \"mm\",\n            \"m\",\n            \"ss\",\n            \"s\",\n            \"x\",\n        ],\n        value: Union[str, bytes, SupportsInt, bytearray],\n        parts: _Parts,\n    ) -> None: ...  # Each token is matched and the corresponding value is parsed and added to the `_Parts` dictionary.\n\n        :param token: The token to parse.\n        :type token: Any\n        :param value: The value of the token.\n        :type value: Any\n        :param parts: A dictionary to update with the parsed values.\n        :type parts: _Parts\n        :raises ParserMatchError: If the hour token value is not between 0 and 12 inclusive for tokens \"a\" or \"A\".\n\n        \"\"\"\n        if token == \"YYYY\":\n            parts[\"year\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"YY\":\n            value = int(value)\n            parts[\"year\"] = 1900 + value if value > 68 else 2000 + value\n\n        elif token in [\"MMMM\", \"MMM\"]:\n            # FIXME: month_number() is nullable\n            parts[\"month\"] = self.locale.month_number(value.lower())  # type: ignore[typeddict-item]\n\n        elif token in [\"MM\", \"M\"]:\n            parts[\"month\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"DDDD\", \"DDD\"]:\n            parts[\"day_of_year\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"DD\", \"D\"]:\n            parts[\"day\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"Do\":\n            parts[\"day\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"dddd\":\n            # locale day names are 1-indexed\n            day_of_week = [x.lower() for x in self.locale.day_names].index(\n                value.lower()\n            )\n            parts[\"day_of_week\"] = day_of_week -
